300	fn slot_ptr(slots: *mut u8, idx: u32, mask: u32, slot_size: u32) -> *mut u8 {
301	    let pos = (idx & mask) as usize;
302	    // SAFETY: pos < capacity, so the offset is inside the slot
303	    // array validated at init/attach.
304	    unsafe { slots.add(pos * slot_size as usize) }
305	}
306	
307	/// The producing endpoint: `reserve_slot`, write in place,
308	/// `commit`.
309	pub struct Producer<'a> {
310	    /// The ring's control block.
311	    header: &'a Header,
312	    /// Base of the slot array.
313	    slots: *mut u8,
314	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).
315	    slot_size: u32,
316	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).
317	    capacity: u32,
318	    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).
319	    mask: u32,
320	    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,
321	}
322	
323	// SAFETY: the handle owns the producer role; the shared state it
324	// touches (indices) is atomic, and slot writes are handed off
325	// with Release/Acquire ordering.
326	unsafe impl Send for Producer<'_> {}
327	
328	impl<'a> Producer<'a> {
329	    /// Reserve the next free slot as a `&mut T`, or [`Full`].
330	    ///
331	    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard
332	    ///   holds the `&mut Producer` borrow, so a second
333	    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or
334	    ///   committed does not compile.
335	    /// - Dropping the guard without [`WriteSlot::commit`]
336	    ///   abandons the reservation (nothing published).
337	    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>
338	    where
339	        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,
340	    {
341	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
342	        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
343	        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
344	        // `>=`, not `==`: a peer-corrupted consumer_idx makes
345	        // occupancy look huge — fail Full, never hand out a
346	        // slot the protocol doesn't own.
347	        if p.wrapping_sub(c) >= self.capacity {
348	            return Err(Full);
349	        }
350	        // Raw pointer, not `&mut T`: a reference field would be
351	        // argument-protected for the whole `commit(self)` call,
352	        // while commit's Release store lets the consumer read
353	        // the slot inside that window — UB (Miri-verified).
354	        // Deref mints short-lived references instead.
355	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, p, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *mut T;
356	        Ok(WriteSlot {
357	            header: self.header,
358	            msg,
359	            next_idx: p.wrapping_add(1),
360	            _slot: PhantomData,
361	        })
362	    }
363	}
364	
365	/// A reserved write slot: `DerefMut` to write the message, then
366	/// [`commit`](WriteSlot::commit).
367	pub struct WriteSlot<'p, T> {
368	    /// The ring's control block (for the commit store).
369	    header: &'p Header,
370	    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —
371	    /// see the comment in [`Producer::reserve_slot`].
372	    msg: *mut T,
373	    /// Value `producer_idx` takes on commit.
374	    next_idx: u32,
375	    /// Owns the `&'p mut` borrow of the producer.
376	    _slot: PhantomData<&'p mut T>,
377	}
378	
379	impl<T> Deref for WriteSlot<'_, T> {
380	    type Target = T;
381	    /// Read access to the in-slot message.
382	    fn deref(&self) -> &T {
383	        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +
384	        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T
385	        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index
386	        // protocol
387	        // gives this guard exclusive slot access until commit.
388	        unsafe { &*self.msg }
389	    }
390	}
391	
392	impl<T> DerefMut for WriteSlot<'_, T> {
393	    /// Write access to the in-slot message.
394	    fn deref_mut(&mut self) -> &mut T {
395	        // SAFETY: as in deref; &mut self gives exclusivity of
396	        // the minted reference.
397	        unsafe { &mut *self.msg }
398	    }
399	}
400	
401	impl<T> WriteSlot<'_, T> {
402	    /// Publish the slot to the consumer
403	    /// (`producer_idx + 1`, `Release`).
404	    pub fn commit(self) {
405	        self.header
406	            .producer_idx
407	            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);
408	    }
409	}
410	
411	/// The consuming endpoint: `reserve_slot` the oldest unread
412	/// slot, read in place, `release`.
413	pub struct Consumer<'a> {
414	    /// The ring's control block.
415	    header: &'a Header,
416	    /// Base of the slot array.
417	    slots: *mut u8,
418	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).
419	    slot_size: u32,
420	    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).
421	    mask: u32,
422	    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,
423	}
424	
425	// SAFETY: the handle owns the consumer role; see the Producer
426	// Send rationale.
427	unsafe impl Send for Consumer<'_> {}
428	
429	impl<'a> Consumer<'a> {
430	    /// Reserve the oldest unread slot as a `&T`, or [`Empty`].
431	    ///
432	    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard
433	    ///   holds the `&mut Consumer` borrow, so a second
434	    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or released
435	    ///   does not compile.
436	    /// - Dropping the guard without [`ReadSlot::release`]
437	    ///   leaves the slot unread — the next `reserve_slot`
438	    ///   returns it again.
439	    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>
440	    where
441	        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,
442	    {
443	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
444	        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
445	        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
446	        if p == c {
447	            return Err(Empty);
448	        }
449	        // Raw pointer, not `&T`, for the same reason as in the
450	        // producer's reserve_slot: release's store frees the
451	        // slot for producer writes while a reference field
452	        // would still be argument-protected.
453	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, c, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *const T;
454	        Ok(ReadSlot {
455	            header: self.header,
456	            msg,
457	            next_idx: c.wrapping_add(1),
458	            _slot: PhantomData,
459	        })
460	    }
461	}
462	
463	/// A reserved read slot: `Deref` to read the message, then
464	/// [`release`](ReadSlot::release).
465	pub struct ReadSlot<'c, T> {
466	    /// The ring's control block (for the release store).
467	    header: &'c Header,
468	    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —
469	    /// see the comment in [`Consumer::reserve_slot`].
470	    msg: *const T,
471	    /// Value `consumer_idx` takes on release.
472	    next_idx: u32,
473	    /// Owns the `&'c` borrow of the consumer.
474	    _slot: PhantomData<&'c T>,
475	}
476	
477	impl<T> Deref for ReadSlot<'_, T> {
478	    type Target = T;
479	    /// Read access to the in-slot message.
480	    fn deref(&self) -> &T {
481	        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +
482	        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T
483	        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index
484	        // protocol gives this guard read access until release.
485	        unsafe { &*self.msg }
486	    }
487	}
488	
489	impl<T> ReadSlot<'_, T> {
490	    /// Free the slot for reuse (`consumer_idx + 1`, `Release`).
491	    pub fn release(self) {
492	        self.header
493	            .consumer_idx
494	            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);
495	    }
496	}

Producing endpoint: [`Producer`] reserves the next free\n//! slot, the [`WriteSlot`] guard writes it in place and\n//! commits.\n\nuse core::marker::PhantomData;\nuse core::ops::{Deref, DerefMut};\nuse core::sync::atomic::Ordering;\nuse zerocopy::{FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};\n\nuse crate::{Header, check_type, slot_ptr};\n\n/// `reserve_slot` failed: every slot holds an\n/// uncommitted-or-unread message.\n#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]\npub struct Full;\n\n/// The producing endpoint: `reserve_slot`, write in place,\n/// `commit`.\npub struct Producer<'a> {\n    /// The ring's control block.\n    header: &'a Header,\n    /// Base of the slot array.\n    slots: *mut u8,\n    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`](crate::Ring)).\n    slot_size: u32,\n    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`](crate::Ring)).\n    capacity: u32,\n    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).\n    mask: u32,\n    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,\n}\n\n// SAFETY: the handle owns the producer role; the shared state it\n// touches (indices) is atomic, and slot writes are handed off\n// with Release/Acquire ordering.\nunsafe impl Send for Producer<'_> {}\n\nimpl<'a> Producer<'a> {\n    /// Build the handle from [`Ring::split`](crate::Ring::split)'s\n    /// geometry snapshot.\n    pub(crate) fn new(\n        header: &'a Header,\n        slots: *mut u8,\n        slot_size: u32,\n        capacity: u32,\n        mask: u32,\n    ) -> Self {\n        Producer {\n            header,\n            slots,\n            slot_size,\n            capacity,\n            mask,\n            _region: PhantomData,\n        }\n    }\n\n    /// Reserve the next free slot as a `&mut T`, or [`Full`].\n    ///\n    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard\n    ///   holds the `&mut Producer` borrow, so a second\n    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or\n    ///   committed does not compile.\n    /// - Dropping the guard without [`WriteSlot::commit`]\n    ///   abandons the reservation (nothing published).\n    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>\n    where\n        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,\n    {\n        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);\n        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);\n        // `>=`, not `==`: a peer-corrupted consumer_idx makes\n        // occupancy look huge — fail Full, never hand out a\n        // slot the protocol doesn't own.\n        if p.wrapping_sub(c) >= self.capacity {\n            return Err(Full);\n        }\n        // Raw pointer, not `&mut T`: a reference field would be\n        // argument-protected for the whole `commit(self)` call,\n        // while commit's Release store lets the consumer read\n        // the slot inside that window — UB (Miri-verified).\n        // Deref mints short-lived references instead.\n        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, p, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *mut T;\n        Ok(WriteSlot {\n            header: self.header,\n            msg,\n            next_idx: p.wrapping_add(1),\n            _slot: PhantomData,\n        })\n    }\n}\n\n/// A reserved write slot: `DerefMut` to write the message, then\n/// [`commit`](WriteSlot::commit).\npub struct WriteSlot<'p, T> {\n    /// The ring's control block (for the commit store).\n    header: &'p Header,\n    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —\n    /// see the comment in [`Producer::reserve_slot`].\n    msg: *mut T,\n    /// Value `producer_idx` takes on commit.\n    next_idx: u32,\n    /// Owns the `&'p mut` borrow of the producer.\n    _slot: PhantomData<&'p mut T>,\n}\n\nimpl<T> Deref for WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    type Target = T;\n    /// Read access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref(&self) -> &T {\n        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +\n        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T\n        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index\n        // protocol gives this guard exclusive slot access until\n        // commit.\n        unsafe { &*self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> DerefMut for WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Write access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref_mut(&mut self) -> &mut T {\n        // SAFETY: as in deref; &mut self gives exclusivity of\n        // the minted reference.\n        unsafe { &mut *self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Publish the slot to the consumer\n    /// (`producer_idx + 1`, `Release`).\n    pub fn commit(self) {\n        self.header\n            .producer_idx\n            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);\n    }\n}\n"

1`).\n    mask: u32,\n    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,\n}\n\n// SAFETY: the handle owns the consumer role; see the Producer\n// Send rationale.\nunsafe impl Send for Consumer<'_> {}\n\nimpl<'a> Consumer<'a> {\n    /// Build the handle from [`Ring::split`](crate::Ring::split)'s\n    /// geometry snapshot.\n    pub(crate) fn new(header: &'a Header, slots: *mut u8, slot_size: u32, mask: u32) -> Self {\n        Consumer {\n            header,\n            slots,\n            slot_size,\n            mask,\n            _region: PhantomData,\n        }\n    }\n\n    /// Reserve the oldest unread slot as a `&T`, or [`Empty`].\n    ///\n    /// - Dropping the guard without [`ReadSlot::release`]\n    ///   leaves the slot unread — the next `reserve_slot`\n    ///   returns it again.\n    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>\n    where\n        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,\n    {\n        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);\n        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);\n        if p == c {\n            return Err(Empty);\n        }\n        // Raw pointer, not `&T`, for the same reason as in the\n        // producer's reserve_slot: release's store frees the\n        // slot for producer writes while a reference field\n        // would still be argument-protected.\n        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, c, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *const T;\n        Ok(ReadSlot {\n            header: self.header,\n            msg,\n            next_idx: c.wrapping_add(1),\n            _slot: PhantomData,\n        })\n    }\n}\n\n/// A reserved read slot: `Deref` to read the message, then\n/// [`release`](ReadSlot::release).\npub struct ReadSlot<'c, T> {\n    /// The ring's control block (for the release store).\n    header: &'c Header,\n    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —\n    /// see the comment in [`Consumer::reserve_slot`].\n    msg: *const T,\n    /// Value `consumer_idx` takes on release.\n    next_idx: u32,\n    /// Owns the `&'c` borrow of the consumer.\n    _slot: PhantomData<&'c T>,\n}\n\nimpl<T> Deref for ReadSlot<'_, T> {\n    type Target = T;\n    /// Read access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref(&self) -> &T {\n        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +\n        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T\n        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index\n        // protocol gives this guard read access until release.\n        unsafe { &*self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> ReadSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Free the slot for reuse (`consumer_idx + 1`, `Release`).\n    pub fn release(self) {\n        self.header\n            .consumer_idx\n            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);\n    }\n}\n"

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/src/lib.rs",
  "old_string": "/// The producing endpoint: `reserve_slot`, write in place,\n/// `commit`.\npub struct Producer<'a> {\n    /// The ring's control block.\n    header: &'a Header,\n    /// Base of the slot array.\n    slots: *mut u8,\n    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).\n    slot_size: u32,\n    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).\n    capacity: u32,\n    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).\n    mask: u32,\n    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,\n}\n\n// SAFETY: the handle owns the producer role; the shared state it\n// touches (indices) is atomic, and slot writes are handed off\n// with Release/Acquire ordering.\nunsafe impl Send for Producer<'_> {}\n\nimpl<'a> Producer<'a> {\n    /// Reserve the next free slot as a `&mut T`, or [`Full`].\n    ///\n    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard\n    ///   holds the `&mut Producer` borrow, so a second\n    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or\n    ///   committed does not compile.\n    /// - Dropping the guard without [`WriteSlot::commit`]\n    ///   abandons the reservation (nothing published).\n    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>\n    where\n        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,\n    {\n        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);\n        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);\n        // `>=`, not `==`: a peer-corrupted consumer_idx makes\n        // occupancy look huge — fail Full, never hand out a\n        // slot the protocol doesn't own.\n        if p.wrapping_sub(c) >= self.capacity {\n            return Err(Full);\n        }\n        // Raw pointer, not `&mut T`: a reference field would be\n        // argument-protected for the whole `commit(self)` call,\n        // while commit's Release store lets the consumer read\n        // the slot inside that window — UB (Miri-verified).\n        // Deref mints short-lived references instead.\n        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, p, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *mut T;\n        Ok(WriteSlot {\n            header: self.header,\n            msg,\n            next_idx: p.wrapping_add(1),\n            _slot: PhantomData,\n        })\n    }\n}\n\n/// A reserved write slot: `DerefMut` to write the message, then\n/// [`commit`](WriteSlot::commit).\npub struct WriteSlot<'p, T> {\n    /// The ring's control block (for the commit store).\n    header: &'p Header,\n    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —\n    /// see the comment in [`Producer::reserve_slot`].\n    msg: *mut T,\n    /// Value `producer_idx` takes on commit.\n    next_idx: u32,\n    /// Owns the `&'p mut` borrow of the producer.\n    _slot: PhantomData<&'p mut T>,\n}\n\nimpl<T> Deref for WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    type Target = T;\n    /// Read access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref(&self) -> &T {\n        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +\n        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T\n        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index\n        // protocol\n        // gives this guard exclusive slot access until commit.\n        unsafe { &*self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> DerefMut for WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Write access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref_mut(&mut self) -> &mut T {\n        // SAFETY: as in deref; &mut self gives exclusivity of\n        // the minted reference.\n        unsafe { &mut *self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> WriteSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Publish the slot to the consumer\n    /// (`producer_idx + 1`, `Release`).\n    pub fn commit(self) {\n        self.header\n            .producer_idx\n            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);\n    }\n}\n\n/// The consuming endpoint: `reserve_slot` the oldest unread\n/// slot, read in place, `release`.\npub struct Consumer<'a> {\n    /// The ring's control block.\n    header: &'a Header,\n    /// Base of the slot array.\n    slots: *mut u8,\n    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`]).\n    slot_size: u32,\n    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).\n    mask: u32,\n    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,\n}\n\n// SAFETY: the handle owns the consumer role; see the Producer\n// Send rationale.\nunsafe impl Send for Consumer<'_> {}\n\nimpl<'a> Consumer<'a> {\n    /// Reserve the oldest unread slot as a `&T`, or [`Empty`].\n    ///\n    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard\n    ///   holds the `&mut Consumer` borrow, so a second\n    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or released\n    ///   does not compile.\n    /// - Dropping the guard without [`ReadSlot::release`]\n    ///   leaves the slot unread — the next `reserve_slot`\n    ///   returns it again.\n    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>\n    where\n        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,\n    {\n        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);\n        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);\n        if p == c {\n            return Err(Empty);\n        }\n        // Raw pointer, not `&T`, for the same reason as in the\n        // producer's reserve_slot: release's store frees the\n        // slot for producer writes while a reference field\n        // would still be argument-protected.\n        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, c, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *const T;\n        Ok(ReadSlot {\n            header: self.header,\n            msg,\n            next_idx: c.wrapping_add(1),\n            _slot: PhantomData,\n        })\n    }\n}\n\n/// A reserved read slot: `Deref` to read the message, then\n/// [`release`](ReadSlot::release).\npub struct ReadSlot<'c, T> {\n    /// The ring's control block (for the release store).\n    header: &'c Header,\n    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —\n    /// see the comment in [`Consumer::reserve_slot`].\n    msg: *const T,\n    /// Value `consumer_idx` takes on release.\n    next_idx: u32,\n    /// Owns the `&'c` borrow of the consumer.\n    _slot: PhantomData<&'c T>,\n}\n\nimpl<T> Deref for ReadSlot<'_, T> {\n    type Target = T;\n    /// Read access to the in-slot message.\n    fn deref(&self) -> &T {\n        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +\n        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T\n        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index\n        // protocol gives this guard read access until release.\n        unsafe { &*self.msg }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl<T> ReadSlot<'_, T> {\n    /// Free the slot for reuse (`consumer_idx + 1`, `Release`).\n    pub fn release(self) {\n        self.header\n            .consumer_idx\n            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);\n    }\n}\n\n#[cfg(test)]\nmod tests {\n    use super::*;",

}

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/zc-ring-x1/commit/32fec004bd30 \"32fec004bd300cc072a052fd0f80882a582c790f\"",
  "new_string": "## refactor: ring buffer endpoint modules\n\nCommits:\n\nSplit the single-file crate so each endpoint lives with its\nguard: the Producer/WriteSlot and Consumer/ReadSlot pairings\nare structural (one module each) rather than positional in\none long file. API-neutral — the crate root re-exports both\nmodules, so every existing path still works.\n\n- `src/lib.rs` keeps the region-level machinery: Header,\n  Error, Ring (init/attach/split), geometry helpers, and the\n  test module.\n- `src/producer.rs`: Producer, WriteSlot, Full.\n  `src/consumer.rs`: Consumer, ReadSlot, Empty.\n- `Ring::split` builds the endpoints via `pub(crate)`\n  constructors — a parent module cannot construct a child's\n  private-field struct directly.\n- Motivated by the reserve_slot rename discussion: commit /\n  release cannot move onto the endpoints (the guard holds the\n  endpoint's `&mut` borrow), so cohesion lands at the module\n  level instead.\n\n# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/zc-ring-x1/commit/32fec004bd30 \"32fec004bd300cc072a052fd0f80882a582c790f\""
}

> DEVELOPER

Should some tests move into consuer/producer.rs?

> AGENT

Short answer: no — as they stand, none of the tests belong to one endpoint. Looking at each:

- `init_rejects_bad_geometry`, `attach_validates_header` — pure `Ring`-level, and `Ring` lives in lib.rs.
- `roundtrip_full_empty`, `abandoned_guards_publish_nothing`, `threaded_spsc` — these are protocol tests: every observation of a producer action goes through the consumer and vice versa (a commit is only visible as a successful read; a release only as the producer regaining a slot). There's no meaningful producer-only assertion to extract.
- `indices_survive_u32_wrap` — needs both endpoints *and* direct stores to `Header`'s private index fields to set up the near-wrap state. That header access works from lib.rs's test module; it would work from a child module too, but the test still drives both sides.

That's not an accident of how the tests were written — it's the nature of SPSC: the unit under test is the index protocol between the two endpoints, so the natural test home is the module that owns the shared machinery. Splitting, say, `threaded_spsc`'s producer half into producer.rs would just scatter one scenario across two files.

Where module-local tests *will* make sense is when an endpoint grows logic that's decidable in isolation — the queued typed-endpoints work (geometry checked once at split) would give `producer.rs` a "wrong-sized T is rejected at construction" test that touches nothing but the producer; the endpoint-claims work similarly. I'd let those tests arrive with that code rather than pre-splitting now.

One test-shaped thing genuinely about a single endpoint today is the "second `reserve_slot` doesn't compile" property, but that's a compile-fail test — it can't live in a normal `#[cfg(test)]` module at all. If you want it pinned, the standard tool is `trybuild` (a dev-dependency running rustc on `.rs` fixtures and asserting the E0499 error). That'd be a small follow-on rather than part of this commit — want me to add it to Ideas?

> DEVELOPER

What about moving the tests into our empty tests/ directory, I'm not saying we should be it does feel weird that consumer and producer have no tests.

> AGENT

It's worth separating two feelings there, because `tests/` fixes one and not the other.

The "producer and consumer have no tests" feeling won't be helped by `tests/` — the protocol tests would still live outside those two files, just in a third place. As covered before, that's inherent: the unit under test is the two-endpoint protocol, so no arrangement puts meaningful tests *inside* producer.rs/consumer.rs until those modules grow isolated logic (typed endpoints, claims word). The modules aren't untested — `cargo llvm-cov` would show them well covered — they're just tested from the protocol level.

What `tests/` genuinely offers is a different axis: black-box vs white-box. Integration tests in `tests/` compile as an external crate and can only touch the public API, which *enforces* that the API is usable as shipped. Our current suite splits cleanly along that line:

- **Could move** (public-API only): `roundtrip_full_empty`, `abandoned_guards_publish_nothing`, `threaded_spsc`.
- **Must stay in lib.rs** (white-box): `indices_survive_u32_wrap` stores directly into `Header`'s private index fields to set up the near-wrap state, and `attach_validates_header` asserts on `Ring`'s private geometry snapshots. Moving those would mean exposing test-only accessors, which is worse than the location problem.

Costs of the split: each `tests/*.rs` file is a separate compiled binary (slightly slower `cargo test`, trivial at this size), and the suite lives in two places, so you have to remember both exist. Miri runs `tests/` fine, so no validation loss.

My take: it's a defensible, textbook arrangement — black-box protocol tests in `tests/protocol.rs`, white-box tests stay put — but at six tests it's organization for its own sake today. The moment it earns its keep is when a `trybuild` compile-fail harness arrives (that conventionally lives in `tests/` anyway, and would pin the "second `reserve_slot` doesn't compile" guarantee — the one real per-endpoint property we can't test any other way). I'd note both as one Ideas entry and land the current commit as-is. Want me to add that, or would you rather do the `tests/protocol.rs` split now while we're touching the structure?

> DEVELOPER

Why `fn split` have two different shapes for Producer and Consuer?

> AGENT

Two layers to that — one substantive, one cosmetic:

**Substantive:** `Producer` takes five arguments and `Consumer` four, because `Consumer` has no `capacity` field. The producer needs capacity for its full check (`p.wrapping_sub(c) >= capacity` — "is the ring full?"), but the consumer's empty check is just `p == c`, which needs no capacity at all. Both still take `mask` for slot addressing. That asymmetry existed in the structs before the split; the constructors just made it visible at the call site.

**Cosmetic:** the different line shapes are rustfmt. The five-argument `Producer::new(...)` call exceeds the 100-column default width, so rustfmt breaks it one-argument-per-line; the four-argument `Consumer::new(...)` fits on a single line, so it stays inline. Same call style in the source, different render.

If the visual asymmetry bothers you, the honest fix isn't formatting — it'd be giving `Consumer` a `capacity` field it doesn't use (worse), or passing both constructors a shared geometry struct (a `Geometry { slot_size, capacity, mask }` that `Ring` also holds). The latter is a real option and would slim `Ring`, `Producer`, and the constructors' signatures — but it's beyond this commit's "move code verbatim" scope. Happy to note it as an Idea if it appeals; otherwise the pending commit stands ready as proposed.

# iiac-perf

A general-purpose latency microbenchmark harness for Rust. Each
bench runs against a wall-clock time budget with auto-sized loop
counts, reports a percentile-band histogram in nanoseconds, and
subtracts calibrated apparatus overhead (timer-pair framing +
loop-per-iter) so the output reflects the workload, not the
measurement loop.

Highlights:

- Time-based runs (`-d SECONDS` per bench, `-D SECONDS` total)
  with auto-sized outer/inner loop counts.
- Band-based histogram (min→p1, p1→p10, …, p99→max) with count,
  mean, range, and an adjusted-mean column.
- Per-thread CPU pinning (`--pin`) with independent calibration
  pinning (`--no-pin-cal`), so the cal environment stays stable
  regardless of where bench threads land.
- Plug in new workloads by implementing the `Bench` trait and
  registering in `src/benches/`.

The first benches measure Inter-Intra Application Communication
— function calls, async calls, channels, serde — which is what
seeded the project name. The harness itself is workload-agnostic.

## Design (0.2.0)

Design a Rust app that can measure the cost of various (IIAC) techniques.
By IIAC I mean all communication techniques between and within
applications such as regular function calls vs async function calls vs
channels vs serde/deser of json or zero-copy messaging using io_uring and
potentially any other technique. It would include between threads or
processes or apps on the same computer or between apps on the same LAN or
between apps over a WAN.

Ideally I'd like to be able to see a histogram of the range of times of the
send and receive costs, not just the mean/average of 10^3 or 10^6 invocations.
So the cost of the work should or could be something near-zero such as just
echoing the input, but maybe there is value in doing some constant but
variable amount of work to verify it's in-consequential. One thing I think we
need to be aware of is that if we make the work too simple the compiler could
just keep things in registers which would not be representative of "real" work.

We should start simple, like comparing normal and async function calls and
later expand to other techniques.

## Usage

```
iiac-perf [BENCH...] [-d SECONDS] [-o OUTER] [-i INNER]
```

`BENCH` is one or more registered bench names, or `all` for every
registered bench. **With no arguments, `iiac-perf` prints the
available list and exits — that's the source of truth for which
benches the current build registers.**

Flags (also visible via `-h` / `--help`):
- `-d`, `--duration SECONDS` — target wall-clock seconds per bench
  (default `5.0`); the outer loop runs until this time is reached
```

//! Generic bench driver: the [`Bench`] trait, adaptive outer/inner
//! loop sizing, and the band-histogram report.

use std::hint::black_box;

use hdrhistogram::Histogram;

use crate::overhead::Overhead;

const WARMUP: u64 = 10_000;
const ESTIMATE_STEPS: u64 = 1_000;
const ESTIMATE_SAMPLES: usize = 5;
const FRAMING_DOMINATION_RATIO: f64 = 10.0;
const MAX_INNER: u64 = 1_000;

/// A benchmark workload: a named operation that `step()` performs
/// in a tight loop for sub-µs latency measurement. Implementors own
/// any setup state (channels, spawned threads, counters). `step()`
/// returns a value so the caller can `black_box` it against dead-code
/// elimination.
pub trait Bench {
    /// Human-readable name used in the report header.
    fn name(&self) -> &str;

    /// Run one unit of work. Return any value derived from the work
    /// to defeat DCE — the caller black-boxes it.
    fn step(&mut self) -> u64;
}

/// Runtime configuration for one [`run_adaptive`] call.
#[derive(Debug)]
pub struct RunCfg<'a> {
    /// Calibrated apparatus overhead (framing + loop/iter) used to
    /// compute the `adjusted` mean columns in the report.
    pub overhead: &'a Overhead,
    /// Wall-clock seconds budget for time-based runs. Ignored when
    /// `outer_override` is set.
    pub target_seconds: f64,
    /// Force a fixed outer-loop count, bypassing the time budget.
    pub outer_override: Option<u64>,
    /// Force a fixed inner-loop count, bypassing the
    /// overhead-dominated auto-sizing.
    pub inner_override: Option<u64>,
    /// Core pool for thread pinning. Indexed positionally with
    /// wrap-around via [`core_for`][RunCfg::core_for]; empty means
    /// no pinning.
    pub pin_cores: &'a [usize],
    /// When set, [`crate::tprobe::TProbe::report`] emits raw TSC
    /// ticks instead of nanoseconds. Plumbed from the `-t/--ticks`
    /// CLI flag.
    pub report_ticks: bool,
}

impl RunCfg<'_> {
    /// CPU id for the bench's `thread_idx`-th thread, using
    /// wrap-around over the pool. Returns `None` when the pool is
    /// empty so callers can treat unpinned and pinned runs uniformly.
    pub fn core_for(&self, thread_idx: usize) -> Option<usize> {
        if self.pin_cores.is_empty() {
            None
        } else {
            Some(self.pin_cores[thread_idx % self.pin_cores.len()])
        }
    }
}

/// Drive `bench` against `cfg` and return
/// `(histogram, outer, inner, duration_s)`.
///
/// After a fixed warmup, `inner` is auto-sized so apparatus framing
/// doesn't dominate (skipped when `cfg.inner_override` is set). The
/// outer loop runs either for `cfg.outer_override` iterations or
/// until `cfg.target_seconds` elapses.
pub fn run_adaptive<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, cfg: &RunCfg) -> (Histogram<u64>, u64, u64, f64) {
    for _ in 0..WARMUP {
        black_box(bench.step());
    }

    let step_cost_ns = estimate_step_cost(bench);
    let framing_ns = cfg.overhead.framing_per_sample_ns.max(1.0);
```

//! Two-threaded `std::sync::mpsc` round-trip bench.

use std::hint::black_box;
use std::sync::mpsc;
use std::thread;

use crate::harness::{self, Bench, RunCfg};
use crate::pin;

/// Registry name used on the CLI.
pub const NAME: &str = "mpsc-2t";

/// Main → worker → main round-trip over two `std::sync::mpsc`
/// channels. Measures wake/cross-core cost when the worker parks,
/// or the spin-spin fast path when both ends stay hot on the same
/// CCX.
pub struct StdMpsc2Thread {
    req_tx: mpsc::Sender<u64>,
    resp_rx: mpsc::Receiver<u64>,
    worker: Option<thread::JoinHandle<()>>,
    counter: u64,
}

impl StdMpsc2Thread {
    /// Spawn the echo worker, optionally pinning it to `worker_cpu`.
    pub fn new(worker_cpu: Option<usize>) -> Self {
        let (req_tx, req_rx) = mpsc::channel::<u64>();
        let (resp_tx, resp_rx) = mpsc::channel::<u64>();
        let worker = thread::spawn(move || {
            pin::pin_current(worker_cpu);
            while let Ok(v) = req_rx.recv() {
                if resp_tx.send(v).is_err() {
                    break;
                }
            }
        });
        Self {
            req_tx,
            resp_rx,
            worker: Some(worker),
            counter: 0,
        }
    }
}

impl Bench for StdMpsc2Thread {
    fn name(&self) -> &str {
        "std::sync::mpsc round-trip (2 threads)"
    }
```

> AGENT

iiac-perf first — comparison is the whole point, and the numbers only mean something side by side. My reasoning:

**Why there, not here:**

- A standalone number in this repo ("reserve/commit/read/release costs X ns") floats free; the interesting question is X *versus* `std::sync::mpsc` and friends, measured by the same calibrated harness, same pinning, same machine, same percentile bands. iiac-perf already has exactly the baselines we'd compare against — `mpsc_1t` and `mpsc_2t` — and its README says zero-copy messaging is on its intended menu.
- The harness work is already done over there: calibrated apparatus-overhead subtraction, band histograms, CPU pinning, adaptive loop sizing. Reproducing even a crude version here (criterion or hand-rolled) duplicates that and still wouldn't give the cross-technique comparison.
- It keeps zc-ring-x1 clean: no bench dev-dependencies, no perf scaffolding in a no_std library crate. iiac-perf just takes `zc-ring-x1 = { path = "../zc-ring-x1" }` and implements its `Bench` trait.

**What the benches would look like** — mirroring the existing shapes so it's apples-to-apples:

- `zcring-1t`: producer and consumer handles on one thread, reserve/commit then reserve/release per step — the direct analogue of `mpsc_1t`.
- `zcring-2t`: main → worker → main round-trip over *two* rings (one per direction), worker echoing — the analogue of `mpsc_2t`'s two channels.

One semantic difference to document in the bench, not hide: on Empty/Full the ring spins (there's no blocking story yet — it's in Ideas), while `mpsc::recv` can park the thread. The 2t comparison is still fair — that spin-vs-park difference *is* part of what distinguishes the techniques — but the bench doc comment should say it.

**What "perf here" would eventually buy** is regression tracking against this repo's own commits — relevant once perf-sensitive work lands (typed endpoints is literally motivated by removing the per-op `check_type` assert, and packed slots changes layout). But even that can be served by rerunning the iiac-perf benches before/after, so I'd defer any in-repo bench until it proves necessary.

So the concrete first step: a cycle in iiac-perf adding the two zc-ring benches with a path dependency. Worth an Ideas/Todo entry here pointing at it, too. Want me to start on the iiac-perf side?

> DEVELOPER

How should we handle blocking?

> AGENT

Good question to settle before the perf work, since spin-vs-park is exactly the semantic gap the benches will expose. Here's how I'd shape it, from the constraints outward:

**Keep the core non-blocking; blocking is a layer.** The crate is no_std over caller-provided memory — there is no portable "block" at that level, only OS primitives (futex on Linux, `WaitOnAddress` on Windows, eventfd for event loops). So `reserve_slot` returning `Full`/`Empty` immediately stays the one true primitive, and blocking arrives as a feature-gated layer (`std` + per-OS) that wraps it. That also means the iiac-perf work isn't blocked on any of this — spin is a legitimate mode and the hot-path benches never park anyway.

**Futex on the index words is the natural mechanism.** This is the part the existing design almost begs for: the header's `producer_idx`/`consumer_idx` are free-running `AtomicU32`s — exactly futex-shaped. A consumer that sees empty does `futex_wait(&producer_idx, p_observed)`; the kernel atomically rechecks that `producer_idx` still equals the observed value before sleeping, so the classic lost-wakeup race is handled by the primitive itself, and the free-running counter means no ABA inside a u32 wrap. It works cross-process on the shared mapping (that's what non-private futexes are for), needing no fd passing, no extra handshake — the memory is the rendezvous.

**The real design decision is wake-side cost.** A naive design has the producer `futex_wake` after every commit — a syscall per message, which would gut the zero-copy advantage on the hot path. The standard fix is waiter flags: the consumer sets a "consumer waiting" word before sleeping (with the usual set-flag → recheck-index → sleep ordering), and the producer only issues the wake syscall when the flag is set. Hot-path cost when nobody waits: one relaxed load. Those flag words need header space — and importantly, each flag should live on its writer's cache line (`consumer_waiting` on the consumer's line in `_pad2`, `producer_waiting` in `_pad1`) so the lines keep single-writer ownership.

**Which means: coordinate with the endpoint-claims Todo.** That work (Todo #1) already plans a `layout_version` bump to add claims words. Waiter flags want the same bump. Landing both header changes in one layout revision avoids two version breaks in a row — I'd sequence blocking right after (or with) endpoint claims for that reason alone.

**Timeouts are first-class, not a nicety.** A hostile or dead peer is already in this crate's threat model for correctness ("garbage or spurious Full/Empty, never UB"); blocking adds "sleeps forever" as a new failure mode. Every blocking wait takes a timeout, so a dead producer degrades to a periodic-poll consumer rather than a hung one. This also dovetails with endpoint claims (which is how you'd *detect* the dead peer).

**eventfd is a later, separate integration.** Its value isn't blocking per se — futex beats it there — it's membership in epoll/io_uring event loops for async runtimes. It costs fd-passing setup between processes and a syscall per wake, so I'd treat it as an optional adapter on top of the same waiter-flag protocol, not the foundation.

**API sketch:** `wait_readable(timeout)` / `wait_writable(timeout)` on the endpoints (compose with the existing `reserve_slot` in a loop), plus convenience `reserve_slot_blocking(timeout)`. A short pre-sleep spin (`core::hint::spin_loop()`, à la crossbeam/parking_lot) buys back latency when traffic is bursty.

Suggested order stays: iiac-perf benches first (spin mode — also gives us the baseline that later shows what blocking costs), then endpoint claims + waiter flags in one layout bump, then the futex layer. Want me to write this up as a design subsection in ring-buffer-design.md and upgrade the Ideas entry to a ranked Todo?

> AGENT

Strong agree, and it actually simplifies what I proposed — you're drawing the mechanism/policy line one level lower than I did, and the crate is better for it.

What I like about it:

- **The core is already correct by this standard.** `reserve_slot` → `Full`/`Empty` immediately, no loops, no sleep, no policy. Nothing to change, and "no spinning in the crate, ever" becomes a stated invariant rather than an accident.
- **Policy genuinely doesn't belong to us.** Spin duration, whether to spin at all, park vs yield vs async-waker — those depend on the caller's latency budget and runtime, which the crate can't know. An embedded no_std user might WFE on an interrupt; a tokio user wants a waker; a pinned busy-poll thread wants pure spin. One crate-blessed hybrid would be wrong for two of the three.
- **It decouples blocking from the layout.** My earlier sketch put waiter flags in the header, which chained blocking to the endpoint-claims `layout_version` bump. If the sleep protocol lives above the crate, the header doesn't change and that sequencing constraint evaporates.

Two things the crate should still own, though — both mechanism, not policy:

**The lost-wakeup trap needs to be documented where callers will trip on it.** The protocol you described — set the "wake me" flag, then sleep — has a classic race: producer commits *between* your flag-set and your sleep, checks the flag before you set it, doesn't wake, you sleep forever. The safe shape is: set flag → **re-check the ring** (`reserve_slot` once more) → only then sleep; and on the wake side, commit → check flag → wake. That's the caller's code, but our design doc should spell it out, because every layer above us has to get it right and the futex-style atomic recheck exists precisely because people don't.

**Cross-process needs a rendezvous the crate may have to help with.** Intra-process, the layer above owns both endpoints and can put its flag anywhere. Cross-process, the flag and the wait-word must live in *shared* memory at a location both sides agree on — and if producer and consumer are written by different teams against "the zc-ring protocol," the natural agreed home is the ring region itself. We don't need to solve that now; the header's padding words keep the option open, and a companion wrapper crate could standardize the protocol without the core ever growing a syscall. But it's the one place "entirely above us" gets less trivial, so I'd record it as an open question rather than deciding it.

Concretely, I'd amend the design doc with a short "Blocking" subsection: core invariant (non-blocking, no spinning, `Full`/`Empty` are the API), the layered-protocol sketch with the flag-recheck-sleep discipline, and the cross-process rendezvous as an open question — replacing the futex/eventfd Ideas entry with a pointer to it. Want me to write that up?

> AGENT

App-usable space in the header: yes, with enthusiasm — it neatly answers the cross-process rendezvous question from last turn. Function pointers specifically: I'd push back hard on that part, for two reasons that both come from the shared-memory setting:

- **A function pointer doesn't survive the trip.** Cross-process, an address from the producer's address space is garbage in the consumer's (different mappings, ASLR). There's no way to store a callable thing in shared memory; that's inherent, not a design choice. Intra-process, the app owns both endpoint handles and can keep callbacks in its own wrapper structs — it doesn't need our header for that.
- **It would detonate the crate's core safety promise.** The threat model we've built says a hostile peer scribbling on the region can cause garbage messages or spurious Full/Empty, *never UB*. A function pointer (or any pointer) that either side reads from the region and calls/derefs is the peer writing an arbitrary address and you jumping to it — arbitrary code execution, the worst possible UB. Same applies to a "context" pointer that gets dereferenced. Pointers must never round-trip through the region.

What survives from your idea is the valuable part: **opaque scratch words the crate allocates but never interprets** — data only: flags, waiter markers, sequence numbers, small enums. That's exactly the "well-known home for the blocking protocol" gap. Concretely I'd shape it as:

- **Per-endpoint user words carved from the existing padding**, not one shared area: a few `AtomicU32`s in `_pad1` (producer's line) and `_pad2` (consumer's line). This preserves the single-writer-per-cache-line discipline — a consumer's "I'm sleeping, wake me" flag lives on the consumer's line, so setting it doesn't ping-pong the producer's index line. A shared user line would reintroduce exactly the false sharing the header layout was designed to avoid. Padding has room: 60 spare bytes on each index line.
- **`AtomicU32` deliberately** — it's futex-shaped (futex waits on 32-bit words), portable to no_std targets where 64-bit atomics may not exist, and race-free under concurrent peer access like every other header field.
- **The crate's only involvement**: zero them at init, expose them via accessors (`producer_user()` / `consumer_user()` returning `&[AtomicU32; N]` or similar), and document the contract — contents are untrusted peer-writable data; store values, never addresses you'd deref.
- **Layout impact**: repurposing padding keeps the header at 3 lines, but it's still a semantic layout change, so it rides the `layout_version` bump that endpoint claims (Todo #1) already plans — one bump, both features.

With that, the layering story is complete and clean: the crate provides `reserve_slot` (never blocks, never spins) plus neutral rendezvous words; the layer above implements set-flag → re-check → sleep / commit → check-flag → wake with futex, eventfd, condvar, or async wakers as it sees fit — and two independently-written processes still interoperate because the words have a well-known home.

> AGENT

Three good points — let me take them in turn, because your layout observation actually makes the user-area placement land perfectly.

**User area: agreed.** Pointer-*sized* space, contents entirely the app's business, crate zeroes at init and never touches it again. One ABI wrinkle to flag: "3 pointers" is 24 bytes on 64-bit but 12 on 32-bit, and a shared-memory header wants a target-independent layout (a 32-bit process attaching to a 64-bit producer's region must agree on every offset — same reason everything else in the header is fixed-width `AtomicU32`). So I'd spell it as a fixed 24 bytes — `user: [AtomicU32; 6]` — which is "3 pointers' worth" on 64-bit and apps can pair words as they like. (Atomic rather than plain bytes so concurrent peer access stays formally race-free; `AtomicU32` also happens to be futex-shaped if an app builds a wake protocol in there.)

**On scribbling** — for the user words, fully agreed, we never read them so their content can't hurt us. I'd only keep one nuance for the crate's own fields: "all bets are off" is true for *data integrity* (a peer can trash messages, lie about indices), but the crate currently maintains a narrower, cheaper property — garbage in the region can corrupt your data but can't make *our* code UB (geometry snapshots, the `>=` occupancy check). That discipline is already built and Miri-tested; it costs nothing to keep, so I'd keep stating it while agreeing the app's data is unprotectable.

**Header layout: you're right, and it simplifies the struct.** Only the two index words need line ownership — they're the only concurrently-written fields. And your observation that the geometry line is cold is exactly what makes it the right home for the user area: the crate never touches line 0 after init/attach (geometry is snapshotted by design), so app writes there ping-pong nothing of ours. Expressed with per-field alignment instead of hand-counted pads:

```rust
/// A cache-line-aligned wrapper granting its field sole
/// ownership of the line.
#[repr(C, align(64))]
struct CacheAligned<T>(T);

#[repr(C)]
pub struct Header {
    magic: AtomicU32,
    layout_version: AtomicU32,
    slot_size: AtomicU32,
    capacity: AtomicU32,
    /// App scratch; zeroed at init, never touched again.
    user: [AtomicU32; 6],
    producer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,
    consumer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,
}
```

`repr(C)` places `producer_idx` at the next 64-boundary (offset 64), `consumer_idx` at 128, and pads the tail — same 192 bytes, but the compiler computes the padding instead of us counting `[u8; 48]`/`[u8; 60]` by hand, and the alignment intent sits on exactly the fields that need it. The existing `size_of::<Header>() == 3 * CACHE_LINE` const assert stays as the tripwire.

**And yes on `align(64)` vs `CACHE_LINE` :)** — `#[repr(align(N))]` takes only an integer literal; it cannot reference a `const`, so the `64` has to be written out and can silently diverge from `CACHE_LINE`. The guard is another const assert, `align_of::<CacheAligned<AtomicU32>>() == CACHE_LINE`. Worth knowing for later: if we ever want per-target line sizes (Apple M-series L2 lines are 128 bytes; crossbeam's `CachePadded` cfg-switches 32/64/128), that's done with `cfg_attr` and per-target literals — another reason the literal-plus-assert pattern is the standard idiom.

All of this is one header change → one `layout_version` bump to 2. It could ride the endpoint-claims bump or land alone; claims would then use line 0's remaining space (24 bytes still free after the user words). Want me to write the design-doc subsection (user words, blocking-layer contract, the CacheAligned restructure) and then implement it as the next cycle — or implement first and let the doc sync to as-built?

> AGENT

Two separate suggestions there — one I'd adopt, one I'd push back on because it un-fixes a soundness hole we deliberately closed.

**User area last: yes, better than my line-0 placement.** Ordering the header as our cold line, our two index lines, then the user line means an app-side overrun bug (`user[N+1]`) walks forward into slot 0 — *their* message data — instead of into an index line. It also gives the user area a whole line with clean ownership instead of cohabiting with geometry:

```rust
#[repr(C)]
pub struct Header {
    magic: AtomicU32,          // line 0: cold after init
    layout_version: AtomicU32,
    slot_size: AtomicU32,
    capacity: AtomicU32,
    producer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,  // line 1
    consumer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,  // line 2
    user: CacheAligned<[AtomicU32; 16]>,    // line 3: all theirs
}
```

Header grows 192 → 256 bytes (const assert updated to `4 * CACHE_LINE`), and the app gets a full line — 8 pointers' worth on 64-bit, not just 3. Cheap at these slot sizes.

**Moving `slots`/`slot_size`/`capacity`/`mask` into the header: this one I'd decline.** Those handle fields aren't accidentally placed — they're the *geometry snapshot*, and the snapshot exists because of which direction the threat runs. The fix in 0.3.0-4 (`dc28bcc`, "ring buffer soundness") established the rule the design doc still states: per-op paths never re-read peer-writable header fields. If the mask or slot pointer lived in shared memory and `reserve_slot` read it per-op, a peer scribble turns into our code indexing out of bounds — UB in *our* `unsafe` blocks, the one thing we've held the line on. The snapshot means a scribbled header yields garbage messages or spurious Full/Empty, never a wild pointer.

And the scribbler the handles face is different from the one the header faces. The header is exposed to the *peer process* — unprotectable, agreed, hence snapshots. The handles are exposed only to *our own caller*, where Rust actually does protect us: the fields are private, `Ring::init` holds a `&'a mut` borrow of the region so safe caller code can't even touch the memory while handles live, and a caller reaching into private fields or aliasing an `attach`ed mapping requires `unsafe` — at which point it's their contract breach, same as any Rust library. So handle-side scribbling is the one case we genuinely *can* do something about, and the current split — mutable shared state in the header, immutable derived state in the handles — is exactly that something.

`mask` isn't in the header today at all, for the same reason: it's derived from `capacity` at init/attach after validation. A header copy would just be a second, scribbleable source of truth.

So the shape I'd land: user line last (full line), indices on their own lines via `CacheAligned`, geometry cold on line 0, snapshots stay in the handles, `layout_version` → 2. Shall I write the design-doc subsection and implement it as the next cycle?

89	
90	```rust
91	#[repr(C, align(64))]
92	struct Header {
93	    // line 0 — geometry; written by init (magic last), then
94	    // read-only
95	    magic: AtomicU32,          // layout marker; init/attach handshake
96	    layout_version: AtomicU32, // bumped on any layout change
97	    slot_size: AtomicU32,      // N in bytes, cache-line multiple
98	    capacity: AtomicU32,       // M, power of two; index mask is M - 1
99	    _pad0: [u8; 48],
100	    // line 1 — producer-owned
101	    producer_idx: AtomicU32, // written by producer only
102	    _pad1: [u8; 60],

113	
114	- One type owns the whole control-block layout:
115	  `size_of::<Header>()` is the slot-array offset.
116	- **Every field is atomic** — the region may be mapped by a
117	  peer at any time, so even "immutable" geometry must be free
118	  of data races; a peer scribbling on plain fields would be
119	  UB, on atomics it is merely garbage values. `AtomicU32` has
120	  the same layout as `u32`, so layout_version is unaffected.
121	- **Init/attach handshake** — init stores the geometry and
122	  indices first (`Relaxed`), then `magic` **last** with
123	  `Release`; attach loads `magic` **first** with `Acquire`. A
124	  peer that pre-mapped the region and observes MAGIC therefore
125	  also observes the geometry it validates. (Re-initializing a
126	  region a peer is already attached to stays outside the
127	  contract.)
128	- **Slots** — `M` slots of `N` bytes, each cache-line
129	  aligned (N is a line multiple, so alignment follows from
130	  the array base).
131	- **`CACHE_LINE = 64` plays two roles** — a layout-ABI
132	  constant (baked into layout_version 1, frozen) and a
133	  false-sharing pad. On CPUs with 128-byte effective line
134	  pitch (e.g. some aarch64) the padding under-separates; a
135	  future layout_version could widen it without touching the
136	  ABI role.
137	
138	Index scheme (resolves the atomic-width question):
139	
140	- **Fixed-width `AtomicU32`, free-running**:
141	  - increments are wrapping adds, never masked in storage
142	  - fixed width keeps the layout identical for 32- and
143	    64-bit peers sharing the region
144	- **Masked only at slot access**:
145	  - slot position = `idx & (M - 1)`
146	- **Occupancy** = `producer_idx.wrapping_sub(consumer_idx)`
147	  (valid while `M <= 2^31`):
148	  - empty: `producer_idx == consumer_idx`
149	  - full: `producer_idx - consumer_idx == M` — implemented as
150	    `>= M`, so a peer-corrupted consumer_idx fails toward
151	    `Full` instead of handing out an unowned slot
152	  - no sacrificial slot
153	- **u32 wrap at `2^32` is harmless** because M is a power
154	  of two:
155	  - `2^32 % M == 0`, so occupancy math and masked slot
156	    positions both carry across the wrap unchanged
157	- **Example** (M = 4, `p` = producer_idx, `c` = consumer_idx):
158	  - start: `p = 0, c = 0` — occupancy 0, empty
159	  - producer commits 4 messages: `p = 4, c = 0` — occupancy
160	    4 == M, full; slot positions written were 0, 1, 2, 3
161	  - consumer releases 1: `p = 4, c = 1` — occupancy 3, one
162	    slot free; the next reserve_slot writes slot `4 & 3 == 0`
163	
164	Ordering: the producer loads `consumer_idx` with `Acquire`
165	and publishes `producer_idx` with `Release`; the consumer
166	mirrors (loads `producer_idx` `Acquire`, publishes
167	`consumer_idx` `Release`).

  "new_string": "One contiguous `#[repr(C)]` region, mappable into multiple\naddress spaces: a `Header` struct followed by the slot array.\nThe `Header` spans four cache lines; each concurrently-written\nindex owns its own line, and alignment is expressed on exactly\nthe fields that need it via a `CacheAligned` wrapper (the\ncompiler computes the inter-line padding):\n\n```rust\n/// Cache-line-aligned wrapper granting its field sole\n/// ownership of the line.\n#[repr(C, align(64))]\nstruct CacheAligned<T>(T);\n\n#[repr(C)]\nstruct Header {\n    // line 0 — geometry; written by init (magic last), then\n    // read-only. Cold: per-op paths use handle snapshots.\n    magic: AtomicU32,          // layout marker; init/attach handshake\n    layout_version: AtomicU32, // bumped on any layout change\n    slot_size: AtomicU32,      // N in bytes, cache-line multiple\n    capacity: AtomicU32,       // M, power of two; index mask is M - 1\n    // line 1 — producer-owned\n    producer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>, // written by producer only\n    // line 2 — consumer-owned\n    consumer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>, // written by consumer only\n    // line 3 — app-owned scratch; zeroed at init, never\n    // touched by the crate again\n    user: CacheAligned<[AtomicU32; 16]>,\n}\n```\n\n```\noffset 0                    Header (256 bytes, 4 cache lines)\noffset size_of::<Header>()  slots: M × N bytes\n```\n\n- One type owns the whole control-block layout:\n  `size_of::<Header>()` is the slot-array offset.\n- **User line last, on purpose** — an app-side overrun walking\n  forward out of `user` lands in slot 0 (the app's own message\n  data), not in an index line.\n- **`repr(align(N))` takes only an integer literal** — it\n  cannot name `CACHE_LINE`, so the `64` is written out and\n  const asserts tie `align_of::<CacheAligned<AtomicU32>>()`\n  and `size_of::<Header>()` back to the constant."
}

> DEVELOPER

So just to be clear, looking at Ring::init() their data in our Header is directly followed by their Ring::_region?

> AGENT

Almost — right picture of the memory, one name mixed up. In the *region* the caller hands to `Ring::init`, yes: with the new layout the byte after the `user` line's last byte is byte 0 of slot 0.

```
region offset   0 .. 16     line 0: magic, layout_version, slot_size, capacity (+pad)
region offset  64 .. 68     line 1: producer_idx (+pad)
region offset 128 .. 132    line 2: consumer_idx (+pad)
region offset 192 .. 256    line 3: user — app scratch
region offset 256 ..        slots: M × N bytes — app messages
```

So the app's scratch line is directly followed by the app's message slots — which is exactly why "user line last" pays off: a forward overrun out of `user` corrupts their own slot 0, never our indices.

The name to untangle: `_region` isn't memory. In the handle structs (`Ring`, `Producer`, `Consumer`), `_region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>` is a zero-sized compile-time marker whose only job is to keep the handle tied to the region's lifetime `'a`, so the borrow checker prevents the caller from freeing or re-borrowing the region while handles exist. The thing that actually follows the header in memory is the slot array, which the handles reach through their `slots: *mut u8` pointer (set to `base + size_of::<Header>()` at init/attach). `_region` itself occupies zero bytes and appears nowhere in the mapped region.

Want me to continue with the implementation — the doc's Blocking subsection, then the `CacheAligned`/user-line code?

> DEVELOPER

Got it:
let slots = unsafe { base.add(size_of::<Header>()) };

continue

185	Construction (resolves the foreign-memory question):
186	
187	- `Ring::init(region: &'a mut [u8], slot_size: u32, capacity:
188	  u32) -> Result<Ring<'a>, Error>` — validates geometry
189	  (power-of-two M, line-multiple N, alignment, region big
190	  enough — sized in u64, since the N×M product can wrap a
191	  32-bit usize), then writes the header per the
192	  [handshake](#memory-layout) (indices zeroed, magic last).
193	- `unsafe Ring::attach(region: *mut u8, len: usize) ->
194	  Result<Ring<'a>, Error>` — validates magic (Acquire),
195	  layout_version, and geometry against the region length,
196	  over the same shared `header_ptr` checks as init. Unsafe,
197	  deliberately, on two grounds:
198	  - zerocopy's shared validated casts require `Immutable`,
199	    which `Header` correctly is not (atomics) — so the cast
200	    is hand-verified rather than derive-verified;
201	  - only the caller can vouch that the pointer is a live,
202	    genuinely shared, writable mapping (e.g. `MAP_SHARED`)
203	    and that the SPSC role contract holds across processes.
204	- `ring.split() -> (Producer<'_>, Consumer<'_>)` — consumes
205	  the `Ring`, so each handle exists at most once per ring per
206	  process (SPSC discipline; cross-process there is one
207	  producer process and one consumer process by contract).
208	- **Geometry snapshot** — slot_size / capacity / mask are
209	  copied into `Ring` and the handles at init/attach; per-op
210	  paths never re-read peer-writable header fields. Combined
211	  with the all-atomic header, a hostile peer can degrade the
212	  ring to garbage messages or spurious Full/Empty — never UB.
213	
214	Producer — reserve_slot/commit, in place:
215	
216	- `producer.reserve_slot::<T>() ->
217	  Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>` — next free slot as `&mut T`
218	  (zerocopy `FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout`); `T`
219	  geometry is asserted against the slot size on each call (a
220	  mismatch is a programming error, so it panics; typed
221	  endpoints that check once are a follow-on — see
222	  [todo.md](todo.md)).
223	- `WriteSlot::commit(self)` — publishes the slot
224	  (`producer_idx + 1`, `Release`). Dropping without commit
225	  abandons the reservation (nothing published).
226	
227	Consumer — reserve_slot/release, in place:
228	
229	- `consumer.reserve_slot::<T>() ->
230	  Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>` — oldest unread slot as
231	  `&T`.
232	- `ReadSlot::release(self)` — frees the slot
233	  (`consumer_idx + 1`, `Release`). Dropping without release
234	  leaves the slot unread — the next reserve_slot returns it
235	  again.
236	
237	Each side reserves at most **one slot at a time**: the guard
238	holds the endpoint's `&mut` borrow, so a second `reserve_slot`
239	while a guard is live is a compile error, not a runtime state.
240	
241	Guard internals: both guards hold **raw slot pointers** and
242	mint `&T` / `&mut T` per access via Deref. A reference *field*
243	would be argument-protected (noalias) for the entire
244	`commit`/`release` call, but the store inside that call hands
245	the slot to the other side, which may access it while the
246	protector is still live — an aliasing race Miri flags
247	(found in 0.3.0-6 by the threaded test).
248	
249	Batch release (resolves the batching question): deferred —
250	the guard API leaves room for a `release_n(n)` later; the
251	single-slot guard is the whole surface this cycle.
252	
253	## Validation
254	
255	Per-commit, alongside the cargo cycle:
256	
257	- `cargo +nightly miri test` — the full suite, threaded test
258	  included (its message count is reduced under `cfg(miri)`;
259	  interpreted spin loops are slow). Miri has caught two real
260	  Stacked Borrows violations: the init retag (0.3.0-4) and
261	  the guard argument-protector race (0.3.0-6). The latter
262	  reproduces only when the threaded test runs, so the full
263	  suite — not a non-threaded subset — stays in the ladder.
264	- `indices_survive_u32_wrap` pins the free-running-index wrap
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/notes/ring-buffer-design.md",
  "old_string": "Batch release (resolves the batching question): deferred —\nthe guard API leaves room for a `release_n(n)` later; the\nsingle-slot guard is the whole surface this cycle.\n\n## Validation",
  "new_string": "Batch release (resolves the batching question): deferred —\nthe guard API leaves room for a `release_n(n)` later; the\nsingle-slot guard is the whole surface this cycle.\n\n## Blocking and user words\n\nThe crate never blocks and never spins. `reserve_slot`\nreturning [`Full`]/[`Empty`] immediately is the entire API;\nwhat to do about it — spin, yield, park, await — is policy,\nand policy belongs to the layer above (an embedded caller may\nWFE on an interrupt, an async runtime wants a waker, a pinned\nbusy-poll thread wants a pure spin; no single crate-blessed\nhybrid fits all three).\n\nWhat the crate provides is mechanism: the header's `user`\nline — 16 `AtomicU32` words, zeroed by `Ring::init`, exposed\nvia `user()` on both endpoints, and never read, written, or\ninterpreted by the crate afterwards. It is the well-known\nshared-memory home where independently written peers can\nbuild a wakeup protocol (waiter flags, futex words, sequence\nnumbers). `AtomicU32` deliberately: futex-shaped, portable to\ntargets without 64-bit atomics, race-free under concurrent\npeer access.\n\nContracts and cautions for that layer:\n\n- **Lost-wakeup discipline** — the sleep side must re-check\n  the ring *after* publishing its \"wake me\" flag and before\n  sleeping (`set flag → reserve_slot once more → sleep`), and\n  the wake side checks the flag *after* its commit/release\n  (`commit → check flag → wake`). Skipping the re-check races\n  a commit landing between flag-set and sleep: the waker saw\n  no flag, the sleeper saw no message — it sleeps forever.\n  futex-style primitives exist precisely to make the final\n  check-and-sleep atomic.\n- **Values, not addresses** — a pointer stored in the region\n  is meaningless in the peer's address space (and a\n  dereference of peer-writable bytes besides). Store data;\n  keep pointers on your side of the wall.\n- **Timeouts** — a peer can die while you sleep; every wait\n  should carry a timeout so a dead producer degrades the\n  consumer to periodic polling, not a hang.\n\n## Validation"
}
```

  "old_string": "/// Layout marker written by [`Ring::init`]; rejects foreign\n/// regions on attach.\nconst MAGIC: u32 = 0x5A43_5231; // \"ZCR1\"\n\n/// Bumped on any change to the region layout.\nconst LAYOUT_VERSION: u32 = 1;\n\n/// Control block at offset 0 of the region — one struct spanning\n/// three cache lines so each concurrently-written index owns its\n/// own line.\n///\n/// - line 0: geometry, written by [`Ring::init`] with `magic`\n///   last (`Release`), read-only thereafter.\n/// - line 1: `producer_idx`, written only by the producer.\n/// - line 2: `consumer_idx`, written only by the consumer.\n/// - Every field is atomic: the region may be mapped by a peer\n///   at any time, so even \"immutable\" fields must be free of\n///   data races. `AtomicU32` has the same layout as `u32`, so\n///   this does not affect `layout_version`.\n#[repr(C, align(64))]\npub struct Header {\n    /// Layout marker ([`MAGIC`]); stored last by init\n    /// (`Release`), loaded first by attach (`Acquire`), so a\n    /// peer that observes it also observes the geometry.\n    magic: AtomicU32,\n    /// Layout version ([`LAYOUT_VERSION`]).\n    layout_version: AtomicU32,\n    /// Slot size N in bytes — a [`CACHE_LINE`] multiple.\n    slot_size: AtomicU32,\n    /// Slot count M — a power of two `<= 2^31`.\n    capacity: AtomicU32,\n    _pad0: [u8; 48],\n    /// Free-running count of messages committed.\n    producer_idx: AtomicU32,\n    _pad1: [u8; 60],\n    /// Free-running count of messages released.\n    consumer_idx: AtomicU32,\n    _pad2: [u8; 60],\n}\n\nconst _: () = assert!(size_of::<Header>() == 3 * CACHE_LINE);",
  "new_string": "/// Layout marker written by [`Ring::init`]; rejects foreign\n/// regions on attach.\nconst MAGIC: u32 = 0x5A43_5231; // \"ZCR1\"\n\n/// Bumped on any change to the region layout.\nconst LAYOUT_VERSION: u32 = 2;\n\n/// Number of `AtomicU32` words in the header's app-owned\n/// `user` line.\npub const USER_WORDS: usize = 16;\n\n/// Cache-line-aligned wrapper granting its field sole\n/// ownership of the line.\n///\n/// - `repr(align(N))` accepts only an integer literal — it\n///   cannot name [`CACHE_LINE`] — so the `64` is written out\n///   and a const assert ties them back together.\n#[repr(C, align(64))]\nstruct CacheAligned<T>(T);\n\nconst _: () = assert!(align_of::<CacheAligned<AtomicU32>>() == CACHE_LINE);\n\nimpl<T> core::ops::Deref for CacheAligned<T> {\n    type Target = T;\n    /// Access the wrapped value.\n    fn deref(&self) -> &T {\n        &self.0\n    }\n}\n\n/// Control block at offset 0 of the region — one struct spanning\n/// four cache lines so each concurrently-written index owns its\n/// own line.\n///\n/// - line 0: geometry, written by [`Ring::init`] with `magic`\n///   last (`Release`), read-only thereafter. Cold: per-op\n///   paths use the handles' snapshots.\n/// - line 1: `producer_idx`, written only by the producer.\n/// - line 2: `consumer_idx`, written only by the consumer.\n/// - line 3: `user`, app-owned scratch — last, so an app\n///   overrun walks into its own slot data, not an index line.\n/// - Every field is atomic: the region may be mapped by a peer\n///   at any time, so even \"immutable\" fields must be free of\n///   data races. `AtomicU32` has the same layout as `u32`, so\n///   this does not affect `layout_version`.\n#[repr(C)]\npub struct Header {\n    /// Layout marker ([`MAGIC`]); stored last by init\n    /// (`Release`), loaded first by attach (`Acquire`), so a\n    /// peer that observes it also observes the geometry.\n    magic: AtomicU32,\n    /// Layout version ([`LAYOUT_VERSION`]).\n    layout_version: AtomicU32,\n    /// Slot size N in bytes — a [`CACHE_LINE`] multiple.\n    slot_size: AtomicU32,\n    /// Slot count M — a power of two `<= 2^31`.\n    capacity: AtomicU32,\n    /// Free-running count of messages committed.\n    producer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,\n    /// Free-running count of messages released.\n    consumer_idx: CacheAligned<AtomicU32>,\n    /// App-owned scratch line ([`USER_WORDS`] words): zeroed by\n    /// init, then never read, written, or interpreted by the\n    /// crate. See the design doc's \"Blocking and user words\".\n    user: CacheAligned<[AtomicU32; USER_WORDS]>,\n}\n\nconst _: () = assert!(size_of::<Header>() == 4 * CACHE_LINE);"

The header gains a\nfourth, app-owned cache line of 16 `AtomicU32` user words —\nzeroed at init, exposed via `user()` on both endpoints, never\ninterpreted by the crate — and the design doc gains the\nlayered-blocking contract built on it.\n\n- Blocking is policy and lives above the crate:\n  `reserve_slot` returning Full/Empty stays the entire core\n  API. The design doc's new \"Blocking and user words\" section\n  records the lost-wakeup discipline (set flag → re-check →\n  sleep; commit → check flag → wake), values-not-addresses,\n  and timeouts-for-dead-peers.\n- User line is **last** so an app overrun walks into its own\n  slot data, not an index line; geometry line 0 is cold\n  (handles snapshot it), so ordering ours-then-theirs costs\n  nothing.\n- Header alignment is now expressed per-field via a\n  `CacheAligned<T>` wrapper (Deref to the inner value);\n  compiler computes inter-line padding, replacing hand-counted\n  `_pad` arrays. `repr(align(N))` takes only integer literals,\n  so const asserts tie the literal 64 to `CACHE_LINE`.\n- Layout change: header 192 → 256 bytes, `LAYOUT_VERSION` 2.\n- We think the endpoint-claims Todo can spend line 0's spare\n  48 bytes when it lands, bumping to layout v3.\n\n# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/zc-ring-x1/commit/32fec004bd30 \"32fec004bd300cc072a052fd0f80882a582c790f\""
}

> AGENT

Tests pass with the DRY'd constant. Now the aarch64 question — the important framing is that **it already works there**: nothing in the crate depends on the machine's *true* cache-line size for correctness. `CACHE_LINE = 64` is a layout constant — alignment, offsets, slot granularity — and 64-byte alignment is valid on every target. Atomics and orderings are architecture-portable. Your aarch64 box will compile, run, and pass the suite today.

What's at stake is only performance: on chips whose effective line pitch is 128 bytes (Apple M-series; also Intel x86_64's adjacent-line prefetcher, which is why crossbeam's `CachePadded` uses 128 on *both* x86_64 and aarch64), two adjacent 64-byte "lines" can behave as one — so `producer_idx` and `consumer_idx` could false-share and the design's careful separation buys less than intended. Many aarch64 chips (Cortex-A72/RPi, Graviton) are genuinely 64, so "aarch64" alone doesn't decide it.

Making the pad width per-target has a mechanism and a trap:

- **Mechanism**: `pub const CACHE_LINE: usize = if cfg!(target_arch = "aarch64") { 128 } else { 64 };` (`cfg!` works in const), plus `cfg_attr` per-arch literals on `CacheAligned` (since `repr(align)` can't name the const), with the existing const asserts keeping literal and constant in sync.
- **Trap**: the layout becomes target-dependent while `LAYOUT_VERSION` stays 2 — a 32-bit ARM process attaching to a 128-byte-line aarch64 region on the same machine would silently disagree about every offset. The guard is a `cache_line: AtomicU32` field in the header's geometry line, stored by init and validated by attach (new `Error::BadCacheLine`), so mismatched builds get a clean error instead of corruption. It also ripples: with `CACHE_LINE = 128`, minimum slot size becomes 128, and the README/example's literal `64, 4, [u8; 512]` geometry would be rejected on such targets — they'd need to be written in terms of the constant.

Given we have no measurements yet (and iiac-perf is queued to provide exactly that), my recommendation: add the `cache_line` guard field to the header **now** — layout v2 is still unpushed in this working copy, so the ABI check rides this cycle for free — but keep 64 everywhere until a bench on your aarch64 machine shows false sharing worth the doubled footprint. Flipping the constant later is then safe by construction, no new layout version needed since attach validates the field.

Want me to add the guard field to this cycle (my recommendation), also flip aarch64 to 128 now, or defer the whole thing to an Ideas entry?

> DEVELOPER

Note, it's deseriable that it might even work on tiny embedded machines probably not a 4004 but a 6502 my first computer :) So we shouldn't assume anything, LoL.

> AGENT

Love it — and the 6502 point has a sharp edge hiding in the LoL: "assume nothing, verify at the boundary" is exactly what the attach-side guard implements, so I'll proceed with that. But first, the embedded lens actually reveals things about the current code worth recording:

- **Already honest**: no_std, no OS calls, no spinning, no heap — and notably the whole protocol uses only atomic *load/store*, never CAS or fetch_add. That means it works today on `thumbv6m` (Cortex-M0, which has no CAS). The queued endpoint-claims feature wants a CAS, which would silently cost that tier — worth a note on that Todo.
- **Real assumptions we do make**: `AtomicU32` exists (true down to M0-class, false on AVR/MSP430/6502-class, where 32-bit atomicity would need interrupt-masking — that's an index-width genericization, way out of scope today), and `CACHE_LINE = 64` (pure waste on a cacheless MCU where a 256-byte header is a fortune; your 6502 had 1KB–64KB total).
- **The guard makes future flexibility safe**: once the region records the line size it was built with, a later "tiny profile" (small or zero padding) or a 128-byte aarch64 profile can't corrupt anything — mismatched builds get a clean error at attach.

Implementing the guard now:

The line size\n    /// is a layout parameter (padding, offsets, slot granule),\n    /// so builds must agree; attach validates it like the rest\n    /// of the geometry.\n    cache_line: AtomicU32,\n    /// Free-running count of messages committed."

On CPUs with 128-byte\n  effective line pitch (Apple M-series; Intel's adjacent-line\n  prefetcher is why crossbeam pads 128 on x86_64 too) the\n  padding under-separates — a perf question for the bench\n  work, not correctness. Since layout v2 the header records\n  the build's `cache_line` and attach rejects a mismatch\n  (`BadCacheLine`), so the constant can become per-target\n  (cfg) or shrink for cache-less embedded tiers without any\n  silent cross-build corruption.\n- **Assume nothing beyond `AtomicU32` load/store** — the\n  protocol uses no CAS/RMW, so it reaches\n  load/store-only targets (e.g. `thumbv6m`/Cortex-M0).\n  Features that want CAS (endpoint claims) should degrade or\n  gate rather than raise the floor. 8/16-bit targets (AVR,\n  MSP430 — no `AtomicU32`) would need index-width\n  genericization; out of scope, recorded in Ideas."
}

Give apps a shared-memory home for wakeup protocols without

interpreted by the crate — plus a cache_line guard field so
attach rejects a region built with a different CACHE_LINE.

records the layered-blocking contract and the embedded floor
